### Direct writes: the destination field does not route

The assembler refuses `aluN` as a destination, so the word is `MAD r2.l*, u0,
#1, #0` as assembled (part1 `0x00012800`, part0 `0x3F41F200`) with the
destination field, part1 bits 15..21, rewritten to 24..27. The disassembler
reads the patched word back as `MAD alu0.l*, u0, #1, #0`, so the field is where
the table says it is.

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `dist-raw-orig` | `0x00000010` | the unpatched raw word writes `r2.lo`: the hex form behaves like the symbolic one |
| `dist-raw-read` | `0x00000000` | that write read back as a row register in the same packet |
| `dist-row` | `0x00000000` | the patched word with nothing reading it: `r2` is untouched, so the write did not go to the row |
| `dist-alu0-same` | `0x00000010` | the patched write issued from slot 0, read from slot 1 as `alu0` |
| `dist-alu1-same`, `dist-alu2-same`, `dist-alu3-same` | `0x00000000` | the same word with the field at 25, 26, 27, read as that latch from slot 1 |
| `dist-slot1-dest24-alu1` | `0x00000010` | the field at 24, issued from slot 1, read as `alu1` |
| `dist-slot1-dest25-alu1` | `0x00000010` | the field at 25, issued from slot 1, read as `alu1` |
| `dist-slot1-dest24-alu0` | `0x00000000` | the field at 24, issued from slot 1, read as `alu0` |
| `dist-alu0-next` | `0x00000010` | the patched write one batch later, `alu0` from slot 0 |

Two things follow. The destination codes 24..27 are accepted by the hardware
and write no row register. They do not choose which latch the value appears
on: `dist-slot1-dest24-alu1` and `dist-slot1-dest25-alu1` read the same value
while `dist-slot1-dest24-alu0` reads zero, so the *issuing slot* decides, and
the field's value only means "no row register" to the writeback.

### What this settles

* a latch read inside a packet is a forward from that slot's result, either
  order, own slot excluded, format-specific;
* one packet boundary inside a group keeps all four latches, a second boundary
  keeps none;
* a group flush keeps `alu0` read from slot 0 and nothing else;
* codes 24..27 are legal destinations that write no row register; the latch a
  read sees is the issuing slot's, not the code's;
* section 9's "read zero" was the bare-read and batch-distance shape, not a
  property of the register.

### What this does not settle

* why only slot 0's `alu0` survives a flush - a register file read rather than
  a latch is the obvious guess and is not measured;
* the physical form: four latches with byte enables, or a bidirectionally
  forwarded write bus plus one saved register;
* whether destination codes 28..31 or an embedded-constant destination behave
  like 24..27;
* what `(send)`/`(recv)` do to a latch read; the assembler's five-operand form
  sets recv and was not used here;
* two-packet carry at buffer sizes other than 3, and any carry across two
  groups.

One configuration is a known hang and was dropped: a four-batch program at
`alu_buffer_size` 3 leaves an incomplete group and the draw times out twice at
20 s. The template has six batches so size 3 has whole groups, and the six-batch
form returns the same words as the four-batch form did for every variant that
existed at that point.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-latch` and `verify-alu-latch` hold 124 rows
each (40 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`928ef8f26664...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_latch.py`.

## 29. Two DW instructions in one group, on two render targets

`--set dw-two-target` (14 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/dw_two_target.in`,
manifest `asm_characterize.dw_two_target.json`. `alu_buffer_size` 2, so batches
0 and 1 are one group. Batch 0 primes `r0/r1` with payload A (`0x44332211`) and
issues the first DW, batch 1 primes the same pair with payload B
(`0x3D2C1B0A`) and issues the second. A later group fetches one target into
`r0/r1` and stores it to rt1, which is what the runner reads, so each program
is run twice: once reading rt1 and once reading rt4. rt4 is bound with
`GRATE_RT4=1` in every variant.

Payload A and B differ in every byte and name the batch whose row the store
read, and the two targets name the DW. Baselines: with no DW in the group rt1
reads `0x4C4C4C4C` (the draw's clear colour) and rt4 reads `0x00000000`.

| variant | rt1 | rt4 |
|:--|--:|--:|
| `two-a1-b4` (rt1 in batch 0, rt4 in batch 1) | `0x3D2C1B0A` | `0x00000000` |
| `two-a4-b1` (rt4 in batch 0, rt1 in batch 1) | `0x00000000` | `0x3D2C1B0A` |
| `two-a1-b1` (both on rt1) | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` |
| `single-1` (one DW on rt1, batch 0) | `0x3D2C1B0A` | `0x00000000` |
| `single-4` (one DW on rt4, batch 0) | `0x4C4C4C4C` | `0x3D2C1B0A` |
| `single-b-1`, `single-b-4` (one DW in batch 1) | nothing | nothing |

What the words say:

* with two DWs on **different** targets, the first DW's target is written and
  the second is not written at all. Section 3's "a second DW in the group
  suppresses the group's store" therefore holds for one target, not for the
  group: a second DW on another target leaves the first store intact and
  silently drops its own;
* with two DWs on the **same** target nothing is written, which reproduces
  section 3;
* a lone DW in the group's second batch writes nothing on either target, so
  the position rule is not an artefact of the colour target;
* every store that happens reports payload B, the group's *last* row, including
  the DW that sits in batch 0 - the same deferred read section 3 measured.

For a compiler this is an MRT constraint, not a scheduling hint: two output
registers cannot be written by two DW words of one group. The second target
keeps its pre-draw content, and if both words name the same target neither
write appears.

Not settled: why the second word is dropped (decode or arbitration), whether
three DWs behave the same, and whether the first DW is still deferred when the
second is the one that would have stored.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-dw-two-target` and `verify-dw-two-target` hold 46
rows each (14 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`8d4cd01de529...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_dw_two_target.py`.

## 30. Bits 29 and 30 of the ALU schedule word

(Corrected by section 32: bits 0..1 count packets, not instructions. The
measurements here are unaffected.)

`--set sched-bits` (16 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_sched.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_sched.json`. Four shapes, each run with no schedule bit
set and with bit 29, bit 30 and both:

    chain   two links chained through alu0/alu1, then a store
    dw      one ALU write and its store in the same batch
    row     a row write in batch 0 stored by batch 1
    plain   nothing written, one store

The 0x801 stream has one word per batch and the assembler fills only bits 0..1
(packet count) and 2..7 (packet address); everything above is emitted as zero,
so no program can set bits 29 or 30 through the grammar. Section 32 shows the
count is a number of packets, not of instructions. The reach is
`GRATE_ALU_SCHED_OR`, a mask OR-ed into every schedule word as the stream is
emitted. Unset, the stream is unchanged, and the 0x806 complement stream comes
from a different array and is never touched.

The runs prove the mask is applied where it matters: every `chain` variant runs
with `GRATE_DUMP_RAW`, and its own log records the uploaded words.

| mask | schedule words in the run's log |
|:--|:--|
| none | `0x00000001`, `0x00000005` |
| bit 29 | `0x20000001`, `0x20000005` |
| bit 30 | `0x40000001`, `0x40000005` |
| both | `0x60000001`, `0x60000005` |

The complement words stayed `0x00000000` in the same dumps.

| shape | no bit | bit 29 | bit 30 | both |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| chain | `0x00100010` | `0x00100010` | `0x00100010` | `0x00100010` |
| dw | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` |
| row | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` | `0x00000010` |
| plain | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` |

So neither bit changes forwarding, the ALU words a batch executes, or whether
its DW stores, for these shapes.

Why that is not surprising: bits 29..31 of the same word are 0xe0 in the layout
the assembler emits for T114 (`to_114_alu_sched` writes 0xe0 into that byte),
so on this chip they are part of a layout the AR20 stream does not use. What is
measured is that setting them on AR20 changes nothing observable; whether they
mean something on a T114 is untested here.

Not settled: bits 8..28 and 31 individually; whether a bit that only matters
when the address/count fields are also non-default exists; the mask is applied
to every batch at once, so a per-batch effect would be averaged out; and the
blob's own schedule words cannot be inspected because `cgc` needs the
proprietary compiler, which this checkout does not have - the words inspected
here are the assembler's.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-sched` and `verify-alu-sched` hold 52 rows
each (16 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`b58c6c8895e2...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_sched.py`. The hook is
documented in `src/libgrate/grate-asm.c` next to the emission it affects.

## 31. What a group flush carries: a forward, not a register

`--set latch-flush` (13 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_latch.in` at
`alu_buffer_size` 1, manifest `asm_characterize.alu_latch_flush.json`.
Section 28 established *that* one latch read survives a group flush; this set
asks *what* it is, because a register a compiler can stash a value in and a
pipeline forward are different tools.

| hypothesis | prediction |
|:--|:--|
| forwarded | the flush forwards the **previous group's slot-0 result**; a group whose slot 0 wrote nothing carries nothing; one group deep; the writer's row destination is irrelevant |
| row alias | `alu0` at flush distance reads a row register the previous group wrote, so the writer's row index decides |
| register | a real saved register holds until overwritten, so an idle group does not lose it and it lasts more than one group |

The two predictions that separate them are the idle group (`flush-idle`) and
the two-write chain (`flush-two-values`); the row alias is separated by
writing through r5 and r7 instead of r2.

| variant | forwarded | row alias | register | measured |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| `flush-carry` (write r2, read next group) | `0x10` | `0x10` | `0x10` | **`0x00000010`** |
| `flush-carry-r5` | `0x10` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000010`** |
| `flush-carry-r7` | `0x10` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000010`** |
| `flush-two-values` (0.0625 then 0.25, read third group) | `0x40` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000040`** |
| `flush-idle` (all-NOP group between) | `0` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-two-deep` (three groups after the write) | `0` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-writer-s1` | `0` | `0` | `0` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-writer-s3` | `0` | `0` | `0` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-reader-s1` | `0` | `0` | `0` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-row-direct` (row control) | `0x10` | `0x10` | `0x10` | **`0x00000010`** |
| `flush-writer-hi` | `0` | `0` | `0` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-carry-bare` | `0` | `0` | `0` | **`0x00000000`** |
| `flush-busy-middle` (middle group writes through slot 3) | `0` | `0` | `0x10` | **`0x00000000`** |

Every word matches the forwarded reading and the other two have at least one
clean contradiction each: the row alias is dead at `flush-carry-r5`/`-r7`
(the writer's row does not matter) and the register reading dies twice
(`flush-idle` loses the value, `flush-two-values` returns the *newest* slot-0
result rather than an older one).

So a latch read across a group boundary is the same forward the packet
boundary has, one pipeline stage later: it needs slot 0 to have issued an ALU
result in the group just before, it is served to slot 0 only, it is one group
deep, and it is format-specific (`flush-writer-hi` and `flush-carry-bare` are
both zero, matching the within-group rule).

**Consequence for a compiler.** `alu0` is not a spill slot: nothing is saved,
so a value cannot live in a latch across a group boundary. Crossing a group
requires a row register (the `flush-row-direct` control and section 28's
`n1-row`), which is what the assembler already does. The one thing a scheduler
can rely on is that a group's own slot 0 may read the previous group's slot-0
result without a row round trip.

Not settled: why slot 0 alone; whether the same holds on a T114; whether the
"slot 0 wrote" condition is about the slot or about the packet's first
instruction (the template always has four slots, so an ALU0 that is NOP is the
only empty form measured).

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-latch-flush` and `verify-alu-latch-flush` hold
43 rows each (13 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned
from `64bb9bf0b736...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_latch_flush.py`.

## 32. Packing three ALU packets into a batch changes the latch rules

`--set pack` (18 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_pack.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_pack.json`. The template's first batch has three `ALU:`
blocks and the other two have one each, so a count-3 batch and a count-1 batch
sit in the same program and the forwarding rules can be compared with one
variable changed. Every batch is its own group (`alu_buffer_size` 1).

### The count in the 0x801 word is a number of packets

The six-batch template of section 28 uploads 24 ALU instructions (six packets)
and its schedule words are `0x00000001, 0x5, 0x9, 0xD, 0x11, 0x15`: count 1,
address 0..5. A three-block batch emits `0x00000003` and uploads 24 words of
its own (`0x804` with `0x18`). So the count field counts **packets** and the
address field is the packet the batch starts at, four instructions per packet.
Section 30 called bits 0..1 an instruction count; that was wrong, the
measurements there are unaffected.

A batch therefore has up to 12 instruction slots, and the grammar reaches them
with repeated `ALU:` blocks. A fourth block is refused: `fs: line 33: Over 3
ALU instructions per exec batch` - the 2-bit count field is the limit and the
assembler enforces it.

### Measured, with bytes `r0.lo`, `r0.hi`, `r1.lo`, `r1.hi`

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `p0-chain` | `0x00000000` | section 28's chain inside packet 0 of a three-packet batch: dead |
| `p1-chain` | `0x00100010` | the same chain in a one-packet batch of the same program: works |
| `p0-x-alu0` | `0x00100000` | packet 0 slot 0 writes, packet 1 slot 0 reads `alu0` |
| `p0-x-alu1` | `0x00100000` | the same for `alu1` |
| `p0-x-alu2` | `0x00100000` | the same for `alu2` |
| `p0-x-alu3` | `0x00100000` | the same for `alu3` |
| `p0-x-s1` | `0x00100000` | the read issued by slot 1 instead of slot 0: same value |
| `p0-two-pkt` | `0x00000000` | packet 0 writes, packet 2 reads: two boundaries |
| `p0-back` | `0x00000000` | packet 0 reads a latch packet 1 writes |
| `p0-intra-p1` | `0x00000000` | two slots of packet 1, one reading the other's latch |
| `p0-bare` | `0x00000000` | the bare (fp20) form of the packet-to-packet read |
| `p0-hi` | `0x00000000` | packet 0 wrote a high half only |
| `p0-row` | `0x00000000` | a row register written in packet 0, read in packet 1 |
| `chain-3pkt` | `0x00100010` | a three-deep dependent chain, one hop per packet |
| `flush-3blk` | `0x00000000` | the write is in the three-packet batch's first packet, next batch reads |
| `flush-3blk-last` | `0x00100000` | the same with the write in its last packet |
| `flush-1blk` | `0x00100000` | the same with a one-packet writing batch: section 31's shape |
| `direct` | `0x00000000` | the store sees nothing: baseline |

### The model the words support

Three rules, each with a positive and a negative control in the same program:

1. **A one-packet batch keeps section 28's intra-packet rule** - a read is
   served by that packet's slot-N result, either slot order, own slot excluded.
   `p1-chain` (`0x00100010`) is the positive control, `p0-chain` (`0`) the
   negative one, and they differ only in which batch holds the chain.
2. **A batch with more than one packet forwards only from the previous
   packet**: all four latches (`p0-x-alu0..3`), any reading slot (`p0-x-s1`),
   one packet deep (`p0-two-pkt`, `p0-intra-p1`) and forward only
   (`p0-back`). It is still format-specific (`p0-bare`, `p0-hi`) and still not
   the row path (`p0-row`). `chain-3pkt` shows the useful half of it: three
   dependent operations, one per packet, chain without leaving the batch.
3. **The group flush carries the batch's last packet's slot-0 result.** With a
   one-packet batch that is the only packet, which is section 31's finding;
   `flush-3blk-last` carries (`0x10`) and `flush-3blk` does not (`0`), because
   in the second the batch's last packet wrote nothing through slot 0.

### What this means for a compiler

* twelve instructions per batch are available today, through repeated `ALU:`
  blocks - the four-slot packet is not the batch's limit;
* packing changes the liveness rules: with more than one packet there is no
  intra-packet bypass, so a dependent pair must sit in adjacent packets. That
  is a cheap constraint compared with the alternative, because the batch then
  carries a three-deep chain internally instead of leaving it to a row round
  trip or a group boundary;
* the group-boundary carry a scheduler can lean on comes from the batch's
  **last** packet, so a batch that ends with NOPs gains nothing from a write in
  its first packet. `flush-3blk` is the case to avoid.

### Not settled

* why a one-packet batch has the intra-packet rule at all - the pipelining
  guess is that a single-packet batch is issued as one group while longer
  batches are issued packet by packet, and it is not measured;
* count 2 is unreachable with an OR mask (the assembler emits 1), so whether
  two-packet batches follow rule 2 is inferred from three packets, not measured;
* whether a batch with four blocks that the assembler allows through a patched
  count behaves like a wrapped zero or executes part of the fourth packet;
* whether MFU/TEX per-batch counts use the same packet units;
* how `send`/`recv` interact with a multi-packet batch.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-pack` and `verify-alu-pack` hold 58 rows each
(18 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`b53c05466697...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_pack.py`.

## 33. Two DW words in one group: the same rule on the colour, spill and MRT

targets

`--set dw-mrt` (23 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/dw_mrt.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.dw_mrt.json`. Section 29 put the two DWs of one group on rt1
and rt4 - the colour surface the runner reads and the spill surface, both of
them special - so "the first DW's target wins" could have been a property of
that pair rather than of MRT output. This set repeats the arrangement on rt5,
the first index that means MRT output (`5..12 = MRT n+1`, so rt5 is
`gl_FragData[1]`), and on rt4/rt5, with rt4 and rt5 both bound
(`GRATE_RT4=1 GRATE_RT5=1`) in every variant so the draw is identical across
the sweep.

`alu_buffer_size` 2, so batches 0 and 1 are one group: batch 0 primes payload A
(`0x44332211`) and issues the first DW, batch 1 primes B (`0x3D2C1B0A`) and
issues the second. A later group fetches one target into `r0/r1` and stores it
to rt1, and each program is run once per target it needs read, with only the
fetch word different.

### Hypotheses

| hypothesis | prediction |
|:--|:--|
| target-agnostic | first-DW-wins is a rule of the group: in every pair and both orders the first DW's target gets the group's final row and the second target keeps its baseline |
| spill-specific | the rule depends on rt4 being the spill surface, so `spill-*` differs from `mrt-*` in which target survives |
| last-wins | the second DW's target is the one written; already refuted for rt1/rt4 by section 29, this asks whether MRT changes it |

### Baselines

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `none-read1` | `0x4C4C4C4C` | rt1 holds the draw's clear colour |
| `none-read4`, `none-read5` | `0x00000000` | rt4 and rt5 are not cleared: an unwritten target reads zero |

The baselines matter for what a dropped DW can be shown *not* to do: on rt4 and
rt5, zero and "untouched" look the same, but rt1 starts at the clear colour, so
a readback of `0x4C4C4C4C` there is positive evidence that rt1 was not written
at all (neither with the group's row nor with zero).

### Measured

Each row is a program; the two `read` columns are the two runs of it.

| variant | rt1 | rt5 | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `mrt-a1-b5` (DW0 rt1, DW1 rt5) | `0x3D2C1B0A` | `0x00000000` | first DW's target written, second dropped |
| `mrt-a5-b1` (DW0 rt5, DW1 rt1) | `0x4C4C4C4C` | `0x3D2C1B0A` | the same with the order reversed |

| variant | rt4 | rt5 | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `spill-a4-b5` (DW0 rt4, DW1 rt5) | `0x3D2C1B0A` | `0x00000000` | rt4 is not special: the first word wins on the spill pair too |
| `spill-a5-b4` (DW0 rt5, DW1 rt4) | `0x00000000` | `0x3D2C1B0A` | the same with the order reversed |

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `same-1-read1` | `0x00000000` | both DWs on rt1, with rt5 bound: section 29's same-target result, so binding another surface changes nothing |
| `same-5-read5` | `0x00000000` | both DWs on rt5: the same-target rule holds on the MRT target |
| `single-1-read1` | `0x3D2C1B0A` | one DW on rt1: positive control |
| `single-1-read5` | `0x00000000` | the same program read at rt5: untouched-target control |
| `single-5-read5` | `0x3D2C1B0A` | one DW on rt5: positive control for the MRT target |
| `single-5-read1` | `0x4C4C4C4C` | read at rt1: untouched-target control |
| `single-4-read4` | `0x3D2C1B0A` | one DW on rt4: positive control |
| `single-5-read4` | `0x00000000` | the same program read at rt4: untouched-target control |
| `single-b-5-read5` | `0x00000000` | the group's only DW in its second batch, on rt5: the position rule is not about the colour target |
| `pair-a1-b5-read1` | `0x44332211` | DW0 stores `r2/r3` (payload A) to rt1 and DW1 stores `r0/r1` (B) to rt5: the surviving store read the row pair its own word names |
| `pair-a1-b5-read5` | `0x00000000` | the same program read at rt5 |
| `pair-a5-b1-read5` | `0x44332211` | the pair with the targets swapped, same reading |

### What the words say

* **First-DW-wins is a rule of the scheduling group, not of either target.**
  Every pair - colour/MRT, spill/MRT and same-target - behaves like section
  29's rt1/rt4 case, and the reversed orders swap which target receives the
  store. "Specific to the RT1/RT4 combination" is refuted: rt4 is not why the
  first word wins, and rt5 is not treated differently because it is MRT.
* **The dropped word leaves its target exactly as it was.** On rt5 the readback
  is the zero baseline, which cannot distinguish "not written" from "written
  with zero", but on rt1 the readback is the clear colour `0x4C4C4C4C`, not
  `0x00000000`: the second word does not write zeros either.
* **The surviving store reports the group's final contents of the row pair the
  word names.** With both words on `r0/r1` that is payload B, even for the word
  in batch 0, which is section 29. The `pair-` variants name `r2/r3` in the
  first word and `r0/r1` in the second: the store that survives returns A,
  because that pair's last write is in batch 0. So the row content is
  evaluated at the group boundary, per row pair, not copied at issue time.
* **One DW per group is the MRT rule for a compiler.** Two exports cannot share
  a group whether they go to different targets or to the same one; the second
  is dropped silently, so a backend that emits a store per output must give
  each store its own group.

### Not settled

* whether the second word is dropped at decode, at arbitration, or written and
  then overwritten by the first - the readback shows no write, not the stage;
* three or more DWs in one group;
* whether a *first* word that names a class-1 (depth) target also wins over a
  second word naming a colour target;
* whether the arbitration is per group or per packet - the two words here are
  one packet each, so a two-word packet was not measured for DWs;
* T114 is untested; everything here is AR20.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-dw-mrt` and `verify-dw-mrt` hold 73 rows each (23
variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`1c923cadbfec...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_dw_mrt.py`.

## 34. Bits 29 and 30 with one batch masked at a time

`--set sched-at` (35 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_sched_at.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_sched_at.json`. Section 30 set bits 29 and 30 on **every**
0x801 word at once, which cannot separate "the bit is inert" from "the bit
matters only for the packet it is attached to". This set masks one batch at a
time with `GRATE_ALU_SCHED_OR_AT`, a batch index next to the existing mask, so
the other batches keep exactly the word the assembler emits.

`alu_buffer_size` 1 and one packet per batch, so the batch index is also the
packet index and the group index:

    batch 0  producer - the whole shape in `chain`, `dw` and `plain`
    batch 1  consumer - the group-boundary carry in `flush`, the storing
             batch in `row`, nothing in the other three
    batch 2  readback only, part of no shape

Five shapes (`chain`, `flush`, `row`, `dw`, `plain`), each with no mask and with
bits 29, 30 and both applied to batch 0 and then to batch 1. Every variant runs
with `GRATE_DUMP_RAW`, and `tests/grate/asm/dump_sched_words.py` turns each
run's log into the `schedule-words.tsv` recorded with the results.

### The mask reached exactly one batch

The dump of `chain-b0-29`, one run of three batches, one packet each:

| batch | 0x801 word | 0x806 word |
|:--|--:|--:|
| 0 | `0x20000001` | `0x00000000` |
| 1 | `0x00000005` | `0x00000000` |
| 2 | `0x00000009` | `0x00000000` |

The mask is on batch 0's word only, bit 29 and nothing else; the address field
still names packets 0, 1 and 2 (`0x1`, `0x5`, `0x9` are count 1 at addresses 0,
1, 2), so the hook does not perturb the fields the assembler fills. The same
holds in all 35 variants: `b1-*` moves the bit to batch 1's word, the unmasked
runs read `0x1/0x5/0x9`, and **every 0x806 word in every run of the set is
`0x00000000`** - the complement stream is built from a different array and the
mask never touches it.

### Measured

| shape | none | b0:29 | b0:30 | b0:both | b1:29 | b1:30 | b1:both |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| `chain` | `0x00100010` | same | same | same | same | same | same |
| `flush` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same | same | same | same |
| `row` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same | same | same | same |
| `dw` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same | same | same | same |
| `plain` | `0x00000000` | same | same | same | same | same | same |

All seven settings of every shape give the word the shape has with no mask at
all. `chain` keeps its intra-packet forward, `flush` keeps the group-boundary
carry, `row` keeps its row, `dw` keeps its store and `plain` still stores
nothing, so neither bit changes what a batch executes, when it commits, or
whether its DW writes - the last three being what a change in packet count or
address would look like.

### What this settles, and what it does not

There is **no batch-specific effect** of bit 29 or bit 30 on AR20 for these
shapes: masking the producer (batch 0) and the consumer or storer (batch 1)
both give the same readback, so section 30's inertness is not an artefact of
the global mask averaging over batches. This set did *not* mask batch 2 - the
readback batch, which takes no part in any shape - so an earlier version of
this paragraph overstated the coverage; section 35 adds the batch-2 variants
and finds the same nothing there. Whether the bits do something on a T114 is untested -
the hook has a T114 branch that transforms the word with `to_114_alu_sched`,
which writes `0xe0` into that byte itself, but no T114 hardware was involved
here and that path was not exercised.

Still open: bit 31 and bits 8..28 individually; a bit that only matters when
the count or address fields are non-default, e.g. inside a three-packet batch
(section 32); shapes whose result is not visible in the colour readback (a
texture or MFU stream); and what the hardware does with the word, since a dump
shows the upload and not the decode.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-sched-at` and `verify-alu-sched-at` hold 109
rows each (35 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`5327ba31d3c0...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte. Each
directory also holds `schedule-words.tsv`, extracted with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/dump_sched_words.py <run dir>`, which is the per-batch
0x801/0x806 evidence above. Regenerate the manifest with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_sched_at.py`.

## 35. Bits 29 and 30 on the readback batch

`--set b2` (20 variants) from the same `tests/grate/asm/alu_sched_at.in` and
generator, manifest `asm_characterize.alu_sched_b2.json`. Section 34 masked
batches 0 and 1 only; its wording said "the producer, the consumer and a batch
that takes no part", which read as if the batch-2 (readback) word had been
masked too. It had not. This set masks batch 2 for the same five shapes, with
the same unmasked baselines in the same program shape, so the two sets can be
compared directly.

| mask | schedule words in one run's log (`chain-*`) |
|:--|:--|
| none | `0x00000001`, `0x00000005`, `0x00000009` |
| bit 29 on batch 2 | `0x00000001`, `0x00000005`, `0x20000009` |
| bit 30 on batch 2 | `0x00000001`, `0x00000005`, `0x40000009` |
| both on batch 2 | `0x00000001`, `0x00000005`, `0x60000009` |

The mask lands on the third word and nothing else, and every 0x806 word in all
20 variants is `0x00000000`.

| shape | none | b2:29 | b2:30 | b2:both |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| `chain` | `0x00100010` | same | same | same |
| `flush` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same |
| `row` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same |
| `dw` | `0x00000010` | same | same | same |
| `plain` | `0x00000000` | same | same | same |

With batch 0 and 1 (section 34) and now batch 2, all three batches of the
template have been masked and none of them reacts to either bit. That is still
only this chip, these five shapes and these three batch positions: it is not a
claim that the bits are inert in general.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-sched-b2` and `verify-alu-sched-b2` hold 64
rows each (20 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`79730d3843d8...`, with `schedule-words.tsv` from the dumps; re-pinning
reproduces the manifest byte for byte. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_sched_at.py --set b2`. `clean-alu-sched-b2`
is the same manifest run from a checkout of the commit that contains it, with
an empty `git status`, and matches the pinned rows exactly.

## 36. Two-packet batches, and where the latch rotation actually lives

`--set pack2` (32 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_pack2.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_pack2.json`, plus `--set pack3` (13 variants) from the
three-packet template `asm/alu_pack.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_pack3.json` as a direct comparison. Section 32 inferred
the multi-packet rule from three packets and never tested two.

Two `ALU:` blocks in one batch are accepted by the grammar, and the assembler
sets the count from the block count, so `pack2` needs no patched schedule word:
batch 0's word is `0x00000002` (count 2, address 0), batch 1 `0x00000009` and
batch 2 `0x0000000D`, straight from the run's dump. `two-pkt-exec` (packet 1
alone writes into a row the next group stores) returns `0x40`, so the second
packet really executes.

### Two-packet results

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `two-pkt-p0-write` | `0x00000010` | packet 0's write reaches the store |
| `xf-alu0`, `-alu1`, `-alu2`, `-alu3` | `0x00000010` | packet 1 reads each latch packet 0 wrote |
| `xf-read-s1`, `xf-read-s3` | `0x00000010` | the reading slot does not matter |
| `xf-bare`, `xf-fp20`, `xf-hi` | `0x00000000` | the format rules of section 28 hold |
| `back` | `0x00000040` | **packet 0 reads a latch packet 1 writes** |
| `back-s1`, `-s2`, `-s3` | `0x00000040` | the same for all four latches |
| `back-cross-a`, `-cross-b` | `0x00000040` | the latch number, not the read slot, decides |
| `back-unrelated` | `0x00000000` | a different latch is not coupled |
| `back-value` | `0x0000007F` | the value is the writer's |
| `back-bare`, `back-fp20` | `0x00000000` | the format rules apply in this direction too |
| `intra-p1` | `0x00000000` | a packet's own slots do not forward in a two-packet batch |
| `two-writes-read-p0` | `0x00000040` | both write, packet 0's read sees packet 1's |
| `two-writes-read-p1` | `0x00000010` | both write, packet 1's read sees packet 0's |
| `chain-2pkt` | `0x00000010` | one hop across the boundary, the next hop (inside packet 1) dies |
| `chain-1pkt` | `0x00100010` | the one-packet control: both hops work |
| `carry-p1` | `0x00000010` | the last packet's slot-0 result carries to the next group |
| `carry-p0` | `0x00000000` | the first packet's does not |
| `carry-both` | `0x00000040` | when both write slot 0, the last packet's value carries |
| `carry-p1-s1` | `0x00000000` | the carry is read by slot 0 only |
| `row-direct` | `0x00000010` | row control: a row is visible in the next group |
| `none` | `0x00000000` | baseline |

### The rule that fits, and what it does to section 32

A two-packet batch couples its packets **in both directions**, and a packet
never sees its own results. The three-packet comparison separates the two
explanations section 32's shape left open, because its backward test read
`alu1` from slot 0 while slot 1 wrote (a cross-index read) and so could not
distinguish "no backward forward" from "packet 0 looks somewhere else":

| three-packet variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `fwd-same`, `fwd-1` | `0x00000010` | packet 1 sees packet 0, packet 2 sees packet 1 |
| `fwd-two-apart` | `0x00000000` | packet 2 does not see packet 0 |
| `back-same`, `back-cross-a`, `back-cross-b`, `back-two-apart` | `0x00000000` | packet 0 does not see packet 1 |
| `back-from-last` | `0x00000040` | **packet 0 sees packet 2, the batch's last** |
| `both-read-p0` | `0x00000000` | packet 0 sees packet 2, which wrote nothing |
| `both-read-p0-last` | `0x00000040` | packet 0 sees packet 2 even though it wrote slot 0 itself |
| `both-read-p1` | `0x00000010` | packet 1 sees packet 0, not its own write |
| `intra-p1` | `0x00000000` | own packet invisible |

Both packet counts fit one rule: **a latch read in slot S of packet k is served
by packet (k-1) mod M**, where M is the number of ALU packets the group issues,
excluding the reading slot's own result. For M=1 that is the packet itself,
which is exactly the intra-packet bypass of section 28; for M=2 the packets see
each other in both directions; for M=3 they form the cycle 0<-2<-1<-0.

**This revises section 32**, which said the multi-packet rule was "the previous
packet, forward only" and that packet 0 sees nothing. The forward half is right
for packets 1..M-1; the wrap and therefore both two-packet backward rows were
missed because the three-packet backward test changed two variables at once.
Section 32's other findings are unaffected: the count field counts packets, a
batch reaches twelve slots through three blocks, a packet's own slots do not
forward in a multi-packet batch, the read format rules are the same, and the
group-boundary carry comes from the last packet.

### How far the rotation reaches

All of the above puts every packet in one batch. `--set latch-rotate` (16
variants) puts one packet in each of several batches on `asm/alu_latch.in` and
asks the same questions at three `alu_buffer_size` values:

| variant | size | word | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `rot1-fwd` | 1 | `0x00000010` | batch 0 -> batch 1 still forwards |
| `rot1-back`, `rot1-back-2`, `rot1-two-apart` | 1 | `0x00000000` | at size 1 nothing goes backwards or two batches |
| `rot2-fwd`, `rot2-back` | 2 | `0x10`, `0x40` | the two batches of a group see each other |
| `rot3-fwd-adj` | 3 | `0x00000010` | batch 1 -> batch 2 |
| `rot3-two-apart` | 3 | `0x00000000` | batch 0 -> batch 2 |
| `rot3-back` | 3 | `0x00000040` | batch 2 -> batch 0 (the wrap) |
| `rot3-back-adj` | 3 | `0x00000000` | batch 2 -> batch 1 is not the previous packet |
| `rot2-carry`, `rot2-carry-first` | 2 | `0x00000000` | no cross-group read at size 2 |
| `rot3-carry`, `rot3-carry-mid`, `rot3-carry-first` | 3 | `0x00000000` | no cross-group read at size 3 from any batch |

So the rotation is **group-scoped, with M = number of packets in the group**,
not batch-scoped: with one packet per batch, size 2 rotates over the two batches
and size 3 over the three, including the 0<-2 wrap.

That also narrows section 31's group carry. Its `flush-carry` shape put one
packet in each batch at size 1, and the same shape at sizes 2 and 3 returns
`0x00` from every batch of the previous group. On this evidence the "previous
group's last packet's slot-0 result" path is measured **only when a group is a
single batch**, which is the configuration sections 31 and 32 used; a
multi-batch group carries nothing across its flush in the shapes tested. Why
the single-batch group keeps a value that a multi-batch group loses is not
established.

### Not settled

* the mechanism: a rotating register file, a two-phase issue order, or something
  else - only the input/output relation is measured;
* whether the rotation's modulus is the group's packet count or the number of
  packets the buffer has issued so far, which the size-1/2/3 rows cannot
  separate from a flush that resets it;
* why the cross-group read exists at size 1 and not at 2 or 3;
* four-packet groups (size 2 with two-packet batches, size 4) and the wrap there;
* `alu_buffer_size` 2 with two-packet batches, which the grammar allows and this
  set does not cover.

### Recorded runs

`pin-alu-pack2` / `verify-alu-pack2` hold 100 rows each (32 variants, three
repeats, controls) pinned from `4c8a39c59e9d...`; `pin-alu-pack3` /
`verify-alu-pack3` 43 rows each (13 variants) from `f36cfda363fa...`;
`pin-latch-rotate` / `verify-latch-rotate` 52 rows each (16 variants) from
`9aaff734f726...`. Each directory holds `schedule-words.tsv` from the run's own
dumps. Regenerate with `make_alu_pack2.py`, `make_alu_pack3.py` and
`make_latch_rotate.py`. The `clean-alu-pack2`, `clean-alu-pack3` and
`clean-latch-rotate` runs are each manifest run from a checkout of the commit
that contains it, empty `git status`, and all three match their pinned rows
exactly.

## 37. send and recv inside a two-packet batch

`--set send-pack` (12 variants) from the same `asm/alu_pack2.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_send_pack.json`. Notes 13 and 16 established the markers
inside one packet: `(this)` is recv, `(other)` is send, recv adds the previous
slot's raw result, and the transfer pairs are (0,1) (1,0) (1,2) (2,0) (2,3)
(3,0) (3,2). Nothing was measured in a multi-packet batch, and section 32 left
it open.

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `recv-p1-s0`, `recv-p0-s0` | `0x00000010` | recv alone works in either packet, accumulator 0 |
| `send-only-p0` | `0x00000040` | the send marker does not change the sender's own write |
| `send-p1-recv-p1` | `0x00000050` | send and recv still transfer **inside** packet 1 (0.0625 + 0.25) |
| `plain-p1-recv-p1` | `0x00000010` | its unmarked twin: the difference is the marker |
| `send-p0-recv-p1` | `0x00000010` | no accumulation across the packet boundary |
| `plain-p0-recv-p1` | `0x00000010` | unmarked twin, same word |
| `send-p1-recv-p0` | `0x00000010` | no accumulation in the other direction either |
| `send-two-p0` | `0x00000010` | two senders in packet 0 still deliver nothing to packet 1 |
| `send-then-plain-read` | `0x00000010` | a send on the producer does not disturb the latch forward |
| `carry-recv-p1` | `0x00000010` | the carry holds the plain slot-0 result |
| `carry-plain-p1` | `0x00000010` | unmarked twin, same word |

What this says:

* the markers work in **any packet** of the batch, including one that is not the
  first, and a lone recv or send behaves as it does in a one-packet batch;
* they are **confined to their packet**: no send/recv transfer was observed
  across a packet boundary in either direction, with one sender or two, even
  though the latch path does cross (section 36);
* they do not disturb the latch path: a plain read of a latch written by a
  sender still returns the value;
* the group carry holds the slot's own result. A recv in slot 0 has no
  earlier slot in its packet to accumulate from, so no accumulated value can
  reach the carry - that part is structural, not a separate measurement, and it
  is the reason this set cannot test "does the carry take a partial result".

What is not settled: why send/recv do not cross a packet boundary while the
latch path does; whether a recv in slot 1 of packet 1 can receive a send from a
slot 0 of packet 0 through the rotation (the markers' transfer matrix would have
something to say only if the two paths met); and whether the marker bits have a
meaning in the multi-packet dispatch that this readback cannot see.

### Recorded runs

`pin-alu-send-pack` and `verify-alu-send-pack` hold 40 rows each (12 variants,
three repeats, controls) pinned from `7398f3f89521...`, with
`schedule-words.tsv`; `clean-alu-send-pack` is the same manifest from a
checkout of the containing commit and matches the pinned rows. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_send_pack.py`.

## What the runner cost

Five things that produced wrong results first:

* **an unencodable instruction looks like a hardware result.** The first
  `MUL` sweep used four uniforms and every variant exited 1 with
  `fs: line N: Invalid src register`; the runner records that as `exit-error`,
  not as a result, which is the only reason it was not mistaken for a colour.
  Check the status of the first repeat before reading any word.
* **`expected` has to be pinned from a measured run, not from a guess.** Two
  words were written the wrong way round in this session (`0x007F0040` for
  `0x7F000040`) because the DW byte order was applied from the wrong end. Run
  once with `expected` omitted, read `results.tsv`, then pin: the second run is
  the proof, the first is only a measurement. The reverse also bites - a word
  pinned from a run whose *environment* was wrong is worse than no pin, because
  it turns a generator bug into a failing manifest.
* **a shadowed variable in a generator is a hardware mystery.** Adding a
  `--count` override to `make_alu_buffer_matrix.py` reused the loop variable's
  name, so every variant after the first was poked with the first variant's
  count (4, the size-1 count). Sizes 3 and 4 then returned zero from every store
  for a whole run, and at size 3 they hung, and the first conclusion written
  down was "sizes 3 and 4 are not reproducible". They were: the manifest was.
  The fix is one variable name; the lesson is that when one run disagrees with
  another, check what the manifest *actually asked for* (here:
  `python3 -c 'import json,collections; ...'` over `GRATE_POKE`) before
  concluding that the hardware changed its mind.
* **one hang invalidates the rest of the run, not just itself.** A count that
  leaves an incomplete final group (4 batches at size 3) timed out, and every later variant
  in that run timed out too - including variants that pass on their own in a
  fresh run - because the wedged channel does not recover while the manifest is
  still going. The runner reports those as ordinary failures. Keep known-risky
  variants last, or in their own manifest. After a *killed* run the channel
  needs the kernel's 10 s CDMA timeout per wedged job before anything works
  again, and until then every draw fails with `****nvrm_init failed****` from
  the assembler, which looks like a broken device rather than a wedged one.
* **a uniform-driven probe needs the immediate-operand control in the same
  run.** `fs_alu_immediate_control.txt` is that control (it must read
  `0x000000FF`) and it is a control in all three manifests, next to the shipped
  varying case (`0xFF01FF00`), because a board that has lost the constant path
  returns a plausible `f(0)` instead of an error.
